Spectral estimates of dynamically-defined and amenable operator families Thanks: This project was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft [ BE 6789/1-1 to S.B. ] and the second author was partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1855541 (PI - A. Gorodetski) and NSF fellowship award DMS-2213277 .
Siegfried Beckus, Alberto Takase

Abstract
. We consider kernel operators defined by a dynamical system. The Hausdorff distance of spectra is estimated by the Hausdorff distance of subsystems. We prove that the spectrum map is 1 2 \smash{\frac{1}{2}} -Hölder continuous provided the group action and kernel are Lipschitz continuous and the group has strict polynomial growth. Also, we prove that the continuity can be improved resulting in the spectrum map being Lipschitz continuous provided the kernel is instead locally-constant. This complements a 1990 result by J. Avron; P.H.M.v. Mouche; B. Simon establishing that one-dimensional discrete quasiperiodic Schrödinger operators with Lipschitz continuous potentials, e.g., the Almost Mathieu Operator, exhibit spectral 1 2 \smash{\frac{1}{2}} -Hölder continuity. Also, this complements a 2019 result by S. Beckus; J. Bellissard; H. Cornean establishing that d d -dimensional discrete subshift Schrödinger operators with locally-constant potentials, e.g., the Fibonacci Hamiltonian, exhibit spectral Lipschitz continuity. Our work exposes the connection between the past two results, and the group, e.g., the Heisenberg group, needs not be the integer lattice nor abelian.



1.
Introduction

The
Almost Mathieu Operator
(AMO) is the linear operator

H_{\lambda,\alpha,\omega}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

defined by

\textstyle(H_{\lambda,\alpha,\omega}\psi)(n)=\psi(n+1)+\psi(n-1)+2\lambda\cos(2\pi(n\alpha+\omega))\psi(n)

for every
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Here
\lambda\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\alpha\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\omega
are parameters in
\mathbb{R}
;
without loss of generality,

\textstyle\omega\in\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}=[0,1]/{\sim}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Observe the spectrum is a nonempty compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
since the AMO is both bounded and self-adjoint.
The AMO has its origins in solid-state physics and the study of electrons;
the name was introduced by B. Simon in 1982
[
61
]
, but
the operator dates back to 1955 when P.G. Harper
[
37
,
38
]
under the tutelage of R.E. Peierls
published
one of the first descriptions of the spectrum with
\lambda=1
and
\omega=0
by utilizing a tight-binding approximation: electrons in a crystal and in a magnetic field have nondiscrete nonevenly-spaced broadened energy values.
Physicists sought to better understand the spectrum;
M. Azbel in 1964
[
1
]
conjectured and D. Hofstadter in 1976
[
39
]
computationally supported (see Figure
1
) that the spectrum has the characteristic of being either band-like for rational
\alpha
or fractal-like for irrational
\alpha\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Decades later A. Avila and S. Jitomirskaya in 2009
[
9
]
made the final step towards the complete solution of the Ten Martini Problem which sought to confirm the conjectured topological structure of the spectrum: if
\alpha=\frac{p}{q}
is rational, then the spectrum is a disjoint union of at most
q
-many compact intervals; if
\alpha
is irrational, then the spectrum is a Cantor set, i.e., a nonempty compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
not having isolated points nor interior points.

Figure 1.
[
41
]
: Hofstadter’s butterfly

Our motivation: the 1990 result

J. Avron; P.H.M.v. Mouche; B. Simon
[
6
]
considered a general operator family (one-dimensional discrete quasiperiodic Schrödinger operators) containing the AMO and established that the Hausdorff distance
{\dist_{\mathsf{H}}}
of spectra has a square root behavior with respect to the Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathsf{H}}
of subsystems or, equivalently, the spectrum map is
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder continuous.
Define the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}=[0,1]/{\sim}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let the phase
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
and let the frequency
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Endow
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
with the metric

\textstyle d:(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\to[0,+\infty):([\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha_{1}\\
\omega_{1}\end{subarray}],[\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha_{2}\\
\omega_{2}\end{subarray}])\mapsto\max\{\lvert\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\rvert,\lvert\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}\rvert\}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

The (quasiperiodic) dynamical system is
\langle\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},(\mathbb{Z},\tau)\rangle\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
i.e.,
\mathbb{Z}
acts on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
with the group action

\textstyle\tau:\mathbb{Z}\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}:(-n,[\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha\\
\omega\end{subarray}])\mapsto[\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha\\
\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\displaystyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\textstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\scriptstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}\omega\end{subarray}]\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
T_{\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}:\omega\mapsto-\alpha+\omega\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let the potential
v:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous.
The kernel for the operator family (one-dimensional discrete Schrödinger operators with potential
v
) is

\textstyle k_{v}:\mathbb{Z}\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{C}:(m,[\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha\\
\omega\end{subarray}])\mapsto\begin{cases}1&\textup{if}~m\in\{-1,1\}\\
v(\omega)&\textup{if}~m=0\\
0&\textup{else}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}\end{cases}

The operator family is
H_{v,\alpha,\omega}:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
defined by

\textstyle(H_{v,\alpha,\omega}\psi)(n)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{v}(-n+m,-n\cdot_{\tau}[\begin{subarray}{1}\alpha\\
\omega\end{subarray}])\psi(m)=\psi(n+1)+\psi(n-1)+v(\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\displaystyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\textstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\scriptstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle T_{\alpha}$\cr$\phantom{T}{}^{-n}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{T^{-n}_{\alpha}}$\crcr}}}\omega)\psi(n)

for every
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Observe if
v(\omega)=2\lambda\cos(2\pi\omega)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
then we recover the AMO.
Endow the collection
\mathscr{S}
of all left-invariant nonempty closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
with the Hausdorff metric

\textstyle d_{\mathsf{H}}:\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{S}\to[0,+\infty]:(X,Y)\mapsto\max\{\sup_{z\in X}d(z,Y),\sup_{z\in Y}d(z,X)\}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

and endow the collection of all nonempty compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
with the Hausdorff metric
{\dist_{\mathsf{H}}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

The 1990 result
[
6
]
is the following theorem:
if
v:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous, then there exist
\delta>0
and
C>0
such that for each
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\cap\{\text{$\delta$-close}\}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\overline{\bigcup_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}}\spec(H_{v,\alpha_{1},\omega})},\overline{\bigcup_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}}\spec(H_{v,\alpha_{2},\omega})})\leq Cd_{\mathsf{H}}(\{\alpha_{1}\}\times\mathbb{T},\{\alpha_{2}\}\times\mathbb{T})^{\smash{\frac{1}{2}}}=C\lvert\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\rvert^{\smash{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Our motivation: a couple remarks, a sharpness remark, and a list of example applications to come

We remark that
\langle\mathbb{Z},\#\rangle
is a second-countable locally-compact Hausdorff group endowed with a left Haar measure (the counting measure).
Also,
\langle\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},(\mathbb{Z},\tau)\rangle
is a metrizable space endowed with a left continuous action.
The square root behavior is sharp due to
\alpha=\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
in the middle of Figure
1
; see
[
14
,
41
]
.
Indeed, for fixed
n
such as
n=0,1,2,3
in Figure
1
,

\textstyle\alpha=\smash{\frac{p}{q}}\to\smash{\frac{1}{2}}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\sup\spec_{n,0}(H_{1,\alpha,0}^{\textsc{amo}})\asymp\pm\sqrt{16\pi n\lvert\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\rvert-16\pi^{2}n^{2}\lvert\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\rvert^{2}}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
\spec_{n,E}
is the
n
-th band nearby energy
E\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
but the behavior can be linear such as at
\alpha=0^{+}

\textstyle\alpha=\smash{\frac{p}{q}}\to 0^{+}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\sup\spec_{n,4}(H_{1,\alpha,0}^{\textsc{amo}})\asymp 4-2\pi(2n+1)\alpha+\smash{\frac{1}{2}}\pi^{2}(2n^{2}+2n+1)\alpha^{2}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

The 2019 result
[
23
]
and the 2018 result
[
22
]
motivated us to consider a more general operator family
\{A_{x}\}_{x\in Z}
and establish that the Hausdorff distance
\dist_{\mathsf{H}}
of spectra has a quantitative (
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder or Lipschitz) behavior with respect to the Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathsf{H}}
of subsystems.
We will demonstrate that our unified theory applies for the following special cases:

\bullet

one-dimensional discrete quasiperiodic Schrödinger operators (known
[
6
]
).

\bullet

d
-dimensional discrete subshift Schrödinger operators (known
[
23
]
).

\bullet

one-dimensional discrete skew-shift Schrödinger operators (new).

\bullet

one-dimensional discrete limit-periodic Schrödinger operators (new).

We remark that
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is special.
One could consider lattices in a locally compact (Lie) group; see
[
22
]
.

Our work: amenability and continuity

Let
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
be
a second-countable locally-compact Hausdorff group endowed with
a left Haar measure;
let
\ell
be a left-invariant proper
\mathcal{T}
-generating metric on
G
and
define, for
g\in G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\lvert g\rvert\coloneqq\ell(g,e)
and
define, for
r\geq 0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

B(r)\coloneqq\{g\in G:\lvert g\rvert<r\}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
\langle Z,(G,\alpha)\rangle
be
a metrizable space endowed with
a left continuous action;
let
d
be a
\mathcal{T}
-generating metric on
Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
k:G\times Z\to\mathbb{C}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
k
is measurable and

\sup_{x\in Z}\int\lVert k(h,\alpha((\cdot)^{\smash{-1}},x))\rVert_{\infty}dh<+\infty\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define, for
x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

{A}_{x}:L^{2}(G)\to L^{2}(G)
by

\textstyle(A_{x}\psi)(g)=\int k(g^{\smash{-1}}h,g^{\smash{-1}}\cdot_{\alpha}x)\psi(h)dh

for every
\psi\in L^{2}(G)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}g\in G\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
\mathscr{S}
be the collection of all subsystems of
\langle Z,(G,\alpha)\rangle\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define, for
X\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\spec(A_{X})\coloneqq\overline{\bigcup_{x\in X}\spec(A_{x})}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

The dynamically-defined operators
A_{x}
and their spectra are the main objects of study in this paper.
Our attention is on the regularity of the spectrum map
X\mapsto\spec(A_{X})\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Using standard techniques such as working with approximate eigenvectors and utilizing strong continuity, we immediately obtain that if
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is unimodular and
A_{x}
is normal for every
x\in Z
and
k
satisfies both a continuity condition and a decay condition, then
\spec(A_{x})=\spec(A_{X(x)})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
where
X(x)\coloneqq\overline{\{g^{\smash{-1}}x:g\in G\}}
(orbit-closure).
We forgo describing the conditions on
k
in detail, but they can be found within
section
5
.
It suffices to say that stricter conditions on
k
are sufficient to prove the following proposition regarding the spectrum map.

Proposition 1.1
.

Assume
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is unimodular,
A_{x}
is normal for every
x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
and the following.

(i)

\alpha
satisfies a uniform continuity condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0
and for each nonempty compact subset
K
of
G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists
\delta>0
such that

d(y,x)<\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\sup_{g\in K}d(g^{\smash{-1}}y,g^{\smash{-1}}x)<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(ii)

k
satisfies a uniform continuity condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0
and for each nonempty compact subset
F
of
G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists
\delta>0
such that

d(y,x)<\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\int\lvert k(h,y)-k(h,x)\rvert\mathbbm{1}_{F}(h)dh<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(iii)

k
satisfies a uniform decay condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists a nonempty compact subset
F
of
G
such that

\sup_{x\in Z}\int\lVert k(h,(\cdot)^{\smash{-1}}x)\mathbbm{1}_{G\setminus F}(h)\rVert_{\infty}dh<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Fix
X\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Then

\textstyle\lim_{Y\xrightarrow{\smash{\mathscr{S}}}X}\sup_{E\in\spec(A_{X})}\dist(E,\spec(A_{Y}))=0\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

More can be obtained provided the group
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is amenable; see
section
3.1.2
for two definitions.
This is the context of the following theorem which establishes that the spectrum map is continuous.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is unimodular,
A_{x}
is normal for every
x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
and the following.

(i)

\alpha
satisfies a uniform continuity condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0
and for each nonempty compact subset
K
of
G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists
\delta>0
such that

d(y,x)<\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\sup_{g\in K}d(g^{\smash{-1}}y,g^{\smash{-1}}x)<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(ii)

k
satisfies a uniform continuity condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0
and for each nonempty compact subset
F
of
G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists
\delta>0
such that

d(y,x)<\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}{\int\lvert k(h,y)-k(h,x)\rvert\mathbbm{1}_{F}(h)dh}<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(iii)

k
satisfies a uniform decay condition:
for each
\varepsilon>0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
there exists a nonempty compact subset
F
of
G
such that

\sup_{x\in Z}\int\lVert k(h,(\cdot)^{\smash{-1}}x)\mathbbm{1}_{G\setminus F}(h)\rVert_{\infty}dh<\varepsilon\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(iv)

\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is amenable.

Then

\textstyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sup_{X,Y\in\mathscr{S},d_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)<\delta}\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\spec(A_{X}),\spec(A_{Y}))=0\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Remark 1.2.1
.

(a)

In the AMO-like case where
\langle G,\lambda\rangle=\langle\mathbb{Z},\#\rangle
and
\langle Z,(G,\alpha)\rangle=\langle\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},(\mathbb{Z},\tau)\rangle
and
A_{k_{v},x}=H_{v,x}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
the spectrum map is continuous due to Theorem
1.2
.
Indeed, the group
\langle\mathbb{Z},\#\rangle
is both unimodular and amenable and the potential
v
is continuous.
We remark that this corollary—for this specific case—is known; publications of this prior result in the 1980s can be found on page 258 within
[
30
]
and on page 382 within
[
3
]
and on page 2205 within
[
4
]
.

(b)

Theorem
1.2
intersects a 2018 result by S. Beckus; J. Bellissard; G. De Nittis
[
22
]
establishing that the spectrum map is continuous.
Indeed, the étale groupoid within
[
22
]
is assumed to satisfy an amenability condition.
We remark that this prior result implies Theorem
1.2
; our proof is different and significant since Theorem
1.3
—
\gamma
-Hölder continuity—is obtainable.

(c)

If the reader is comfortable with the phase space
Z
being compact, then
(
i
)
immediately holds.
If the reader is comfortable with the potential
v
being discontinuous in the AMO-like case, then
(
ii
)
needs not hold thus
x\mapsto H_{v,x}\psi
needs not be continuous.
We remark that models of quasicrystals have potentials defined on
\mathbb{T}
being indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{[1-\alpha,1)}
in the AMO-like case thus discontinuous in the euclidean topology, and the spectrum map is discontinuous at rational frequencies
\alpha\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
but in 1991
[
15
]
it was established (and plotted) that the spectrum map is continuous at irrational frequencies
\alpha\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
where—in a different perspective—the potential defined on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is ‘evaluation at zero’ thus continuous in the product topology; see
section
2.2
.
If the reader is comfortable with the kernel
k
having compact range, e.g.,
k_{v}
has range
\{-1,0,1\}
in the AMO-like case or the self-adjoint Jacobi operator case
[
63
]
, then
(
iii
)
immediately holds.
If the reader is comfortable with the group
G
being
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or compact or abelian or the Heisenberg group or the lamplighter group, then
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is both unimodular and amenable
(
iv
)
.

More can be obtained provided stricter conditions replace
(
i
–
iv
)
.
This is the context of the following main theorem which establishes that the spectrum map is
\gamma
-Hölder continuous (
\gamma=\smash{\frac{1}{2}}\text{ or }1
).

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume
\langle G,\lambda\rangle
is unimodular,
A_{x}
is normal for every
x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
and the following.

(i)

\alpha
satisfies a Lipschitz continuity condition:
there exists
c_{\alpha}>0
such that for each
g\in G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

d(g^{\smash{-1}}x,g^{\smash{-1}}y)\leq(c_{\alpha}\lvert g\rvert+1)d(x,y)\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(ii)

k
satisfies one of two quantitative continuity conditions considered; see part (
a
) and (
b
) below.

(iii)

k
satisfies a linear decay condition:
there exists
c_{s}>0
such that for each
r\geq 0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\sup_{x\in Z}\int\lVert k(h,(\cdot)^{\smash{-1}}x)\min\{\lvert h\rvert,r\}\rVert_{\infty}dh\leq c_{s}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(iv)

\langle(G,\ell),\lambda\rangle
is strictly-polynomial-growing:
there exist
b\geq 1
and
c_{1}\geq c_{0}>0
such that for each
r\geq 1\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

c_{0}r^{b}\leq\lambda(B(r))\leq c_{1}r^{b}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Then

(a)

If
k
satisfies a Lipschitz continuity condition:
there exists
c_{k}\in L^{1}_{+}(G)
such that for
\lambda
-a.e.
h\in G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\lvert k(h,x)-k(h,y)\rvert\leq c_{k}(h)d(x,y)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
then for all
X,Y\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\leq{\min\{\delta,1\}}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\spec(A_{X}),\spec(A_{Y}))\leq Cd_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
\delta\coloneqq\frac{c_{s}}{\lVert c_{k}\rVert_{1}c_{\alpha}}(\frac{(2+b)^{2+b}c_{1}}{2b^{b}c_{0}})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
C\coloneqq 2(\lVert c_{k}\rVert_{1}c_{\alpha}c_{s}(\frac{(2+b)^{2+b}c_{1}}{2b^{b}c_{0}})^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lVert c_{k}\rVert_{1}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(b)

If
k
satisfies a locally-constant condition:
there exists
c_{k}\in L^{\infty}_{+}(G)
such that for
\lambda
-a.e.
h\in G\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

d(x,y)<\frac{1}{c_{k}(h)}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}k(h,x)=k(h,y)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
then for all
X,Y\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\leq\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\spec(A_{X}),\spec(A_{Y}))\leq Cd_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
\delta\coloneqq\frac{1}{(\lVert c_{k}\rVert_{\infty}+1)(c_{\alpha}+1)}
and
C\coloneqq(\lVert c_{k}\rVert_{\infty}+1)(c_{\alpha}+1)c_{s}(\frac{(2+b)^{2+b}c_{1}}{2b^{b}c_{0}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Remark 1.3.1
.

(a)

Theorem
1.3
(
a
) generalizes the 1990 result by J. Avron; P.H.M.v. Mouche; B. Simon
[
6
]
, and
Theorem
1.3
(
b
) generalizes the 2019 result by S. Beckus; J. Bellissard; H. Cornean
[
23
]
.
Our work exposes the connection between the past two results and reveals that quantitative continuity of the spectrum map follows from quite general operator properties.

(b)

Lipschitz continuity of the action
(
i
)
and strict polynomial growth of the group
(
iv
)
are specific conditions covering a large class of models.
We remark that our proof allows one to weaken or strengthen both conditions.
For example, we will demonstrate that our unified theory applies in
section
2.4
after condition
(
i
)
is weakened so that the group action is instead assumed to be exponential Lipschitz leading to a log-Hölder continuity result.
Also, one can generalize Lemma
4.5
(where the polynomial growth-estimates of the group, e.g., the Heisenberg group, are utilized) so that yet another quantitative continuity result can be obtained for groups with (sub)exponential growth-estimates.
Specifically, Lemma
4.5
is a machine that has as an input the polynomial growth-estimates and that has as an output a suitable family of almost invariant vectors to be utilized later as cutoff functions.
The generalization reduces to replacing growth-estimates of the group with estimates from the suitable family of almost invariant vectors.
We remark that the lamplighter group has exponential growth.

(c)

Theorem
1.3
(
a
) is sharp due to the 1990 works
[
14
,
40
]
; see also
[
18
]
.
We remark that
the regularity can be improved
to be Lipschitz
provided the kernel is instead locally-constant; see Theorem
1.3
(
b
).
Also,
in 1994
[
18
]
it was established that
the regularity at the boundary of a spectral gap can be improved
to be Lipschitz for a general operator family containing the AMO
provided the gap width is locally-positive.
Also,
in 1998
[
43
]
it was established that
the regularity can be improved
to be Lipschitz-log for the AMO
provided the coupling
\lambda
is in the open set
(0,\smash{\frac{2}{29}})\cup(\smash{\frac{29}{2}},+\infty)
; see also
[
44
,
66
]
.
Also,
in 2014
[
45
]
it was established that
the regularity can be generalized
to be
\smash{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}
-Hölder for one-dimensional discrete quasiperiodic Schrödinger operators with
\gamma
-Hölder continuous potentials;
our proofs utilize almost invariant vectors as cutoff functions which is inspired by the 1990 works
[
6
,
27
]
which itself inspired the generalization within
[
45
]
, and we remark that
our regularity can too be generalized
to be
\smash{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}
-Hölder in the setting for Theorem
1.3
(
a
)
provided the kernel is instead
\gamma
-Hölder continuous.

Our work: final remarks

To explain why the continuous behavior of the spectra has been studied for various models, there are cases where one has a sequence of (periodic) approximations and thus a sequence of convergent spectra.
This has been useful to analyze the spectral theory of operators.
For example, rational approximations provide a deeper understanding of the Almost Mathieu Operator and related quasiperiodic models.
Similarly, periodic approximations provide a deeper understanding of the Fibonacci Hamiltonian and related Sturmian models.
For more papers about ‘almost periodic’ operators, we recommend the 1990 paper by A. Sütő
[
62
]
(also
[
17
]
) and the references therein.
Also, we recommend the 2016 paper by D. Damanik; A. Gorodetski; W. Yessen
[
29
]
.
Approximations are a practical tool to compute the spectrum.
Indeed, D. Hofstadter in 1976
[
39
]
plotted one such spectral approximation for the AMO (see Figure
1
), but what is more important is that it can be utilized to derive spectral properties of the limit-operator
A_{x}
from the periodic approximants
A_{y_{j}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Indeed, physicists sought to better understand electrons in a crystal and in a magnetic field;
E.J. Austin and M. Wilkinson in 1990
[
5
]
computationally studied a general operator family containing the AMO and plotted a Hofstadter-like butterfly.
Also, physicists sought to better understand the fractal dimension of
\spec(\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle H_{1,\alpha,0}$\cr$\phantom{H}{}^{\textsc{amo}}$\cr$\displaystyle\phantom{H^{\textsc{amo}}_{1,\alpha,0}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle H_{1,\alpha,0}$\cr$\phantom{H}{}^{\textsc{amo}}$\cr$\textstyle\phantom{H^{\textsc{amo}}_{1,\alpha,0}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle H_{1,\alpha,0}$\cr$\phantom{H}{}^{\textsc{amo}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\phantom{H^{\textsc{amo}}_{1,\alpha,0}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle H_{1,\alpha,0}$\cr$\phantom{H}{}^{\textsc{amo}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{H^{\textsc{amo}}_{1,\alpha,0}}$\crcr}}})
;
M. Kohmoto and C. Tang in 1986
[
48
]
computationally studied
\alpha=(\sqrt{5}-1)/2\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}(\sqrt{2}-1)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}(\pi-3)\sqrt{3}

and conjectured that the dimension is
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
for irrational
\alpha
(the dimension is
1
for rational
\alpha
); see also
[
13
,
33
]
.
Years later
Y. Last in 1994
[
51
]
proved that the Hausdorff dimension does not exceed
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
for topologically-generic irrational
\alpha^{({\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{T}})}
—the proof utilizes
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder regularity of the spectrum map.
But,
E.J. Austin and M. Wilkinson in 1994
[
7
]
proposed that the box-counting dimension is nonconstant for irrational
\alpha
and approaches zero for specific
\alpha_{n}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
and
T. Geisel; R. Ketzmerick; K. Kruse; F. Steinbach in 1998
[
34
]
proposed that, given an assumption, the Hausdorff dimension does not exceed
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
for all irrational
\alpha\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

A decade later
S. Jitomirskaya and I. Krasovsky in 2019
[
46
]
made the final step towards the conjectured fractal dimension of the spectrum: the Hausdorff dimension does not exceed
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
for
all
irrational
\alpha
—the proof utilizes Lipschitz-log regularity of the spectrum map.
We remark that
Y. Last and M. Shamis in 2016
[
54
]
proved that the Hausdorff dimension is
0
for topologically-generic irrational
\alpha^{({\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})}
—the proof utilizes
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder regularity of the spectrum map—and
S. Jitomirskaya and S. Zhang in 2022
[
47
]
proved that the box-counting dimension is
1
for the same topologically-generic irrational
\alpha^{({\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Also,
B. Helffer; Q. Liu; Y. Qu; Q. Zhou in 2019
[
42
]
proved that the collection
\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}
of all irrational
\alpha
such that the Hausdorff dimension is positive (thus
\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\varnothing
) is topologically-dense and itself has positive Hausdorff dimension.

Within
[
10
]
,
periodic Jacobi matrices on trees are studied, and
our theorem does not apply since
we consider groups but they consider trees.
We remark that the
[
10
]
authors themselves remark that
what makes their objects fascinating is that the fundamental group is nonabelian.
Within
[
24
]
,
Schrödinger operators on Lie groups are studied, and
our theorem does not apply since
we consider dynamically-defined operators but they do not have a dynamical system.
Also, their group needs not be unimodular thus needs not have polynomial growth but their group satisfies a weak polynomial growth condition.
We remark that the
[
24
]
authors themselves remark that
what makes their theorem fascinating is that their work applies to the Heisenberg group (a nonabelian group).
Within
[
53
]
,
random Schrödinger operators on manifolds are studied.
We remark that the
[
53
]
authors themselves remark that
their work continues the
[
11
]
approach (see also
[
12
]
), and
what makes their theorem fascinating is that they use techniques from Connes’ noncommutative integration theory and von Neumann algebras.
For more related papers, see
[
28
,
36
]
.

Within
[
11
]
,
our operator families are studied, and
we consider spectral continuity results but they consider the integrated density of states and spectral gap-labeling results.
We remark that the
[
11
]
authors themselves remark that
their work generalizes results by both R. Johnson; J. Moser (1982) and F. Delyon; B. Souillard (1983), and
what makes their theorem fascinating is that they can deal with an arbitrary dimension both in the discrete and in the continuous case.

In
section
2
we demonstrate example applications.
In
section
3
we provide the preliminaries.
In
section
4
we prove the main results.
In
section
5
we prove a standard lemma.

2.
Example Applications

2.1.
One-dimensional Discrete Quasiperiodic Schrödinger Operators

We demonstrate that our unified theory applies for this titular setting.

Let
\langle\mathbb{Z},\#\rangle
be the one-dimensional lattice endowed with the counting measure.
Let
\ell
be the metric on
\mathbb{Z}
defined by

\textstyle\ell(n,m)=\lvert-n+m\rvert

for every
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the phase space
Z\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
d
be the metric on
Z
defined by

\textstyle d(x,y)=\max\{\lvert x_{1}-y_{1}\rvert,\lvert x_{2}-y_{2}\rvert\}

for every
x,y\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
\langle Z,(\mathbb{Z},\tau)\rangle
be the (quasiperiodic) dynamical system defined by

\textstyle-n\cdot_{\tau}x=\left[\begin{subarray}{1}x_{1}\\
nx_{1}+x_{2}\end{subarray}\right]

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define, for
v\in C(\mathbb{T})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
the kernel
k_{v}=k:\mathbb{Z}\times Z\to\mathbb{C}
by

\textstyle k(m,x)=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if}~m\in\{-1,1\}\\
v(x_{2})&\textup{if}~m=0\\
0&\textup{else}\end{cases}

for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the operator family
\{A_{v,x}\}_{v\in C(\mathbb{T}),x\in Z}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}))
by

\textstyle(A_{x}\psi)(n)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}k(-n+m,-n\cdot_{\tau}x)\psi(m)=\psi(n+1)+\psi(n-1)+v(nx_{1}+x_{2})\psi(n)

for every
v\in C(\mathbb{T})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Observe if
v(\omega)=2\lambda\cos(2\pi\omega)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
then we recover the Almost Mathieu Operator.

Theorem 2.1

(
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder regularity)
.

Assume the potential
v:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous, i.e., there exists
\mathrm{c}_{v}=\mathrm{c}>0
such that for all
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{T}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\lvert v(\omega_{1})-v(\omega_{2})\rvert\leq\mathrm{c}\mspace{1muminus1mu}\lvert\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}\rvert\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Then

(a)

\tau
satisfies a Lipschitz continuity condition:
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d(-n\cdot_{\tau}x,-n\cdot_{\tau}y)\leq(\lvert n\rvert+1)d(x,y)\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(b)

k
satisfies a Lipschitz continuity condition:
for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle\lvert k(m,x)-k(m,y)\rvert\leq\mathrm{c}\mspace{1muminus1mu}\delta_{0}(m)d(x,y)\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(c)

k
satisfies a linear decay condition:
for each
r\geq 0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle\sup_{x\in Z}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\lVert k(m,-(\cdot)\cdot_{\tau}x)\min\{\lvert m\rvert,r\}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(d)

\langle(\mathbb{Z},\ell),\#\rangle
is strictly-polynomial-growing:
for each
r\geq 1\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

r\leq\#B(r)\leq 3r\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(e)

For all
X,Y\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\leq{\min\{\delta,1\}}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\spec(A_{X}),\spec(A_{Y}))\leq Cd_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
\delta\coloneqq\frac{2}{\mathrm{c}}(\frac{3^{4}}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
C\coloneqq 2(\mathrm{c}\mspace{1muminus1mu}\smash{\frac{2}{1}}(\frac{3^{4}}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{c}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Theorem
2.1
(
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
) are easy to see and come in handy since Theorem
2.1
(
e
) follows from a combination of (
a
–
d
) and Theorem
1.3
(
a
).
We remark that a 1990 result by both
[
27
]
and
[
6
]

established that the spectrum map is Hölder continuous in this specific setting;
the regularity was established to be
\smash{\frac{1}{3}}
-Hölder
[
27
]
but was improved to be
\smash{\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder
[
6
]
.
Our estimate is different and significant since
\delta=\frac{2}{\mathrm{c}}(\frac{3^{4}}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
is explicitly written; another
C
constant is explicitly written within
[
6
]
.

2.2.
Multi-dimensional Discrete Subshift Schrödinger Operators

We demonstrate that our unified theory applies for this titular setting.

Let
\langle\mathbb{Z}^{d},\#\rangle
be the multi-dimensional lattice endowed with the counting measure.
Let
\ell
be the metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
defined by

\textstyle\ell(n,m)=\max\{\lvert-n_{1}+m_{1}\rvert,\ldots,\lvert-n_{d}+m_{d}\rvert\}

for every
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the phase space
Z\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
d
be the metric on
Z
defined by

\textstyle d(x,y)=\inf\{\smash{\frac{1}{r+1}}:r\in[0,+\infty)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x|_{B(r)}=y|_{B(r)}\}

for every
x,y\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
\langle Z,(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\tau)\rangle
be the (subshift) dynamical system defined by

\textstyle-n\cdot_{\tau}x=(m\mapsto x(n+m))\eqqcolon T^{-n}x

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define, for
v\in C(Z)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
the kernel
k_{v}=k:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times Z\to\mathbb{C}
by

\textstyle k(m,x)=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if}~\lVert m\rVert_{1}=1\\
v(x)&\textup{if}~m=\smash[t]{\vec{0}}\\
0&\textup{else}\end{cases}

for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the operator family
\{A_{v,x}\}_{v\in C(Z),x\in Z}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}))
by

\textstyle(A_{x}\psi)(n)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}k(-n+m,-n\cdot_{\tau}x)\psi(m)=(\sum_{\lVert m\rVert_{1}=1}\psi(n+m))+v(T^{-n}x)\psi(n)

for every
v\in C(Z)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Observe if
v(x)=x(\smash[t]{\vec{0}})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
then we recover the Fibonacci Hamiltonian
[
29
]
.

Theorem 2.2

(Lipschitz regularity)
.

Assume the potential
v:Z\to\mathbb{R}
is locally-constant, i.e., there exist
\mathrm{r}_{v}=\mathrm{r}\geq 0
and
\mathrm{v}_{v}=\mathrm{v}:\{0,1\}^{B(\mathrm{r})}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for each
x\in Z\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

v(x)=\mathrm{v}(x|_{B(\mathrm{r})})\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Then

(a)

\tau
satisfies a Lipschitz continuity condition:
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d(-n\cdot_{\tau}x,-n\cdot_{\tau}y)\leq(\lvert n\rvert+1)d(x,y)\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(b)

k
satisfies a locally-constant condition:
for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle d(x,y)<\smash{\frac{1}{(\mathrm{r}+1)\delta_{0}(m)}}\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}k(m,x)=k(m,y)\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(c)

k
satisfies a linear decay condition:
for each
r\geq 0\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle\sup_{x\in Z}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert k(m,-(\cdot)\cdot_{\tau}x)\min\{\lvert m\rvert,r\}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2d\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(d)

\langle(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\ell),\#\rangle
is strictly-polynomial-growing:
for each
r\geq 1\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

r^{d}\leq\#B(r)\leq 3^{d}r^{d}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

(e)

For all
X,Y\in\mathscr{S}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

\textstyle\hskip 0.0ptd_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\leq\delta\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\Rightarrow\mspace{4muplus0muminus4mu}\dist_{\mathsf{H}}(\spec(A_{X}),\spec(A_{Y}))\leq Cd_{\mathsf{H}}(X,Y)\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}

where
\delta\coloneqq\frac{1}{2(\mathrm{r}+2)}
and
C\coloneqq 2(\mathrm{r}+2)\smash{\frac{2d}{1}}(\frac{(2+d)^{2+d}3^{d}}{2d^{d}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Theorem
2.2
(
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
) are easy to see and come in handy since Theorem
2.2
(
e
) follows from a combination of (
a
–
d
) and Theorem
1.3
(
b
).
We remark that a 2019 result by
[
23
]

established that the spectrum map is Lipschitz continuous in this specific setting.
Our estimate is different and significant since
\delta=\frac{1}{2(\mathrm{r}+2)}
is explicitly written; another
C
constant is explicitly written within
[
23
]
.

2.3.
One-dimensional Discrete Skew-shift Schrödinger Operators

We demonstrate that our unified theory applies for this titular setting provided a lenient condition replaces condition
(
i
)
within Theorem
1.3
; see Theorem
2.3
(
a
).

Let
\langle\mathbb{Z},\#\rangle
be the one-dimensional lattice endowed with the counting measure.
Let
\ell
be the metric on
\mathbb{Z}
defined by

\textstyle\ell(n,m)=\lvert-n+m\rvert

for every
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the phase space
Z\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
d
be the metric on
Z
defined by

\textstyle d(x,y)=\max\{\lvert x_{1}-y_{1}\rvert,\lvert x_{2}-y_{2}\rvert,\lvert x_{3}-y_{3}\rvert\}

for every
x,y\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Let
\langle Z,(\mathbb{Z},\tau)\rangle
be the (skew-shift) dynamical system defined by

\textstyle-n\cdot_{\tau}x=\left[\begin{subarray}{1}x_{1}\\
nx_{1}+x_{2}\\
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}x_{1}+nx_{2}+x_{3}\end{subarray}\right]

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define, for
v\in C(\mathbb{T})\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss,}}{\text{,}}{\text{,}}
the kernel
k_{v}=k:\mathbb{Z}\times Z\to\mathbb{C}
by

\textstyle k(m,x)=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if}~m\in\{-1,1\}\\
v(x_{3})&\textup{if}~m=0\\
0&\textup{else}\end{cases}

for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{,\kern-3.79996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule[-1.94247pt]{1.47774pt}{1.1611pt}}\hss}\kern 2.13324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss{,}}\hskip 1.66669pt\mspace{3muplus0muminus3mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{2muplus0muminus2mu}}{\textup{,}\mspace{1muplus0muminus1mu}}x\in Z\mathchoice{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{.\kern-2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\smash{\rule{1.26663pt}{1.26663pt}}\hss}\kern 2.11108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss.}}{\text{.}}{\text{.}}

Define the operator family
\{A_{v,x}\}_{v\in C(\mathbb{T}),x\in Z}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}))
by

\textstyle(A_{x}\psi)(n)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}k(-n+m,-n\cdot_{\tau}x)\psi(m)=\psi(n+1)+\psi(n-1)+v(\smash{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}x_{1}+nx_{2}+x_{3})\psi(n)

for every
